05 Vale, POSCO, Jinchuan and Indonesian projects

Evidence cut-off: 17 September 2026. This chapter separates plant production, exported products and ownership. Export quantities are consolidated in the main report. An unverified product is not necessarily absent; it lacks sufficient direct evidence for a firm operating classification.

1. Operating conclusions

Project Defensible operating classification Commercial relationship Principal limitation
Vale Sorowako Established high-grade nickel matte producer; low-grade matte is an internal intermediate Vale Canada and Sumitomo Metal Mining offtake system Contract buyer, Japanese consignee and final refinery are distinct
KRS NPI production verified Jinchuan–Tsingshan associated project Common ownership with USMI does not establish matte production
USMI High-grade matte production supported by converter operating evidence Associated supply to Jinchuan Exact intermediate feed suppliers and proportions remain undisclosed
Nicole NPI operating base; high-grade matte investment plan and export records POSCO equity investment and intended materials integration Five export records and undifferentiated revenue do not prove own matte production
MMP RKEF-and-converter project moving through commissioning and sales Domestic capital, Mitsui marketing cooperation A memorandum is not completed equity investment or full-output offtake
BMS Ferronickel verified; nickel sulfate separately classified Kalla industrial diversification; historical POSCO memorandum Sulfate plans do not establish operating matte production
Ceria First ferronickel production in 2025 Domestic mine-to-smelter integration Proposed Glencore investment and future processing must not be treated as completed
ANTAM Established ore and ferronickel businesses; new battery projects at separate stages State-owned resource base plus processing and battery partners CNGR, CBL and legacy ferronickel assets are different projects

2. Vale: an established matte system alongside new hydrometallurgical investments

Why Sorowako supplies the same customers

Sorowako has operated since 1978. Its historic investment rationale was integrated ore processing and overseas refining, rather than a recent conversion of an NPI plant to serve batteries. The relevant saleable product is high-grade matte. Low-grade matte inside the smelting and converting sequence should not be counted again as independent external production. Vale operations

The 2024 ownership transaction illustrates why equity and offtake require separate fields. Following the transaction, MIND ID held 34%, Vale Canada 33.88% and SMM 11.48%; Vale Japan’s former direct interest disappeared. SMM’s 1 July 2024 announcement confirms completion on 28 June and preservation of its rights and obligations to purchase 20% of annual Sorowako production. Its reduced equity interest therefore did not proportionately reduce the contracted share of material. Vale annual report, printed p.20, SMM announcement, p.1

Vale’s sustainability disclosure identifies the approximately 80%/20% Vale Canada/SMM customer arrangement. Customs entries naming Vale Japan can be consistent with this: the contractual customer and processing consignee need not be the same legal entity. Vale also describes an Indonesia–Matsusaka–Clydach processing chain, so shipment to Japan does not establish Japan as the final place of nickel consumption. Continued deliveries are explained by contractual allocation and compatible refining assets, without assuming a speculative spot-trading motive. 2024 sustainability report, Vale Q2 2024 results

Pricing and the 2026 operating change

Historical disclosures describe pricing with reference to LME nickel cash prices and Fastmarkets cobalt prices. They do not justify reconstructing every deduction or contract expiry provision. A significant update appears in Vale Indonesia’s Q1 2026 release: 2026 is the first full year at 82% matte payability. Older assumptions should therefore not be carried forward unchanged. Payability is a pricing parameter, not the product’s nickel assay.

That release also attributes weaker first-quarter output to planned maintenance, including the Furnace 3 rebuild, and the impact of the approved 2026 RKAB. This provides a contemporaneous explanation relevant to the weaker same-period export picture. It is not a complete reconciliation of January–July shipments, inventories and production. A subsequent company public-expose statement reports improved second-quarter output after the rebuild. The evidence supports maintenance followed by recovery, rather than indefinite decline. Q1 2026 release, p.1, company public-expose statement

New projects should not increase the legacy matte total

Pomalaa/KNI, developed with Huayou and Ford, is an HPAL project producing MHP, with planned annual contained metal of 120,000 tonnes nickel and approximately 15,000 tonnes cobalt. Those are not MHP product tonnes. Sorowako limonite and the Morowali/Bahodopi partnership with GEM likewise require separate rows from the traditional matte operation. Pomalaa project

Ownership language also matters. Vale’s Q1 2025 presentation described a 30% PTVI interest for Morowali, but rights to a call option of up to 30% for Pomalaa and Sorowako. An option is not an exercised shareholding. These developments exploit limonite and connect resource ownership, processing expertise and battery demand. Their scale or partner interests do not disclose Ford’s offtake percentage, a contract duration or a pricing formula. Q1 2025 presentation, p.14

3. Sumitomo Metal Mining: equity feed and outside purchases coexist

SMM combines upstream investment, Japanese nickel/cobalt refining and materials production. Its Niihama refinery uses high-grade matte through the MCLE chloride-leaching/electrowinning route. Its 2025 due-diligence disclosure distinguishes Niihama’s matte, mixed-sulfide and crude nickel-sulfate inputs from the different feed configuration at Harima. It is therefore inappropriate to allocate every imported matte cargo automatically to Harima. Nickel refinery, 2025 due-diligence report, p.1

Where customs records establish Lancon or Cosan deliveries to SMM, an external refinery-feed purchase is a defensible interpretation. No named equity relationship or dedicated offtake agreement was located. The refinery’s feed requirements explain why it can purchase this material. They do not establish that a particular contract was intended to replace Vale shipments, undercut other suppliers or secure exclusive supply.

SMM’s December 2024 announcement also envisaged matte production at Hyuga, with construction from 2025 and completion targeted for fiscal 2027. This is a future diversification of supply, not evidence of an operating Japanese matte source during the earlier Indonesian trade history. Hyuga investment announcement

4. Jinchuan: KRS and USMI perform different functions

Ownership does not erase the product distinction

Jinchuan’s activities span resources, nickel/cobalt smelting and materials. Its 2023 review refers to completion of an Indonesian Tsingshan cooperation transaction; a 2024 overseas visit specifically identifies KRS and USMI. The current subsidiary directory lists both as controlled enterprises. These sources support associated-project classification, while leaving precise historical percentages and transaction dates to project-level records. 2023 review, 2024 visit, subsidiary directory

The previously checked rating document distinguished operating NPI at KRS from the then-building USMI matte project. More recent, named converter-operating evidence published in May 2026 describes actual work and heats at USMI. That is materially stronger evidence of own production than a capacity announcement. USMI can therefore be classified as a high-grade matte producer with high confidence, without making the same change to KRS. USMI converter account, 20 May 2026

The exact feed bridge remains incomplete: disclosed association does not establish that KRS supplied every tonne of intermediate to USMI, or that USMI made all its own low-grade matte. These gaps should remain visible, but they need not prevent a conclusion about confirmed converter operation.

Why the material moves to Jinchuan

Associated overseas production provides raw-material security for the group. A tangible domestic outlet is Jinchuan’s nickel-sulfate project commissioned on 27 March 2025. The official account identifies high-grade matte as feed and pressure oxidation/leaching as part of the process. Its 280,000-tonne project description concerns nickel-sulfate product capacity, not contained nickel or imported matte tonnage. Jinchuan project announcement

USMI-to-Jinchuan can thus be marked associated feed supply. Other Indonesian suppliers without verified ownership links remain external procurement. The group also has nickel/cobalt refining uses, so sulfate production is not necessarily the destination of every shipment. Jinchuan Trading, established in Shanghai Lingang in 2021, is a trading entity rather than a separate refinery. Trading company profile

USMI’s stronger 2026 export flow is directionally consistent with established converter activity. It does not quantify production ramp-up without inventory and domestic-sales reconciliation. No publicly verified fixed offtake percentage, duration, payability or minimum lifting obligation was located. “Associated supply” is consequently more precise than an invented long-term full-output contract.

5. Nicole: ownership, commercial revenue and product proof

POSCO’s expansion from steel into battery materials explains the strategic interest in Indonesian intermediates. Its 2023 investment presentation described the Halmahera pyrometallurgical joint venture, a 49% POSCO interest, planned 52,000 tonnes of contained nickel in matte and a 65–75% nickel assay, with completion targeted for 2025. The original intended product was therefore high-grade matte; the plan itself does not certify successful production. POSCO Q2 2023 presentation, p.8

Historical financial statements narrow the commercial timeline. No sales were recorded for 2023 or 2024, and Q1 2025 still showed none. H1 2025 then reported revenue of KRW191,778 million, increasing to KRW491,610 million for nine months. The first verified revenue window is consequently Q2 2025. The reported 2025 total of KRW701,059 million does not separate NPI from matte. Q1 2025 statements, printed pp.20–21, H1 statement mirror, printed p.22, Q3 statements, printed p.23

Together with the previously established NPI operating evidence and five high-grade matte export entries, the appropriate conclusion is: commercial operation and matte sales are established, but own high-grade matte production still needs direct operating confirmation. An absence of corresponding 2026 exports does not establish shutdown; product mix, domestic deliveries, shipment timing or exporter changes remain possibilities rather than findings.

Intended supply into POSCO’s materials system explains the investment logic. It does not disclose a binding output share, minimum quantity, duration, price formula or particular Korean receiving plant. A 49% investment is not evidence of a 49% offtake entitlement. A named first matte heat, product-specific production disclosure or downstream supplier declaration would resolve more than further repetitions of total corporate revenue.

6. MMP and Mitsui: domestic investment with international market access

MMP sits within MMS Solution and MMSGI’s diversification into processing and sustainable businesses. Public company material emphasizes domestic investment. Mitsui’s subsequent involvement therefore should not be used to recast the original smelter as Japanese-controlled. MMSGI history

The company’s 26 June 2025 release confirms power-on and initial commissioning at Kariangau, Balikpapan, with two 48 MVA RKEF units and a converter. A later company update retained full operation in 2026 as a target. Emerging 2026 export sales fit that sequence, but neither a target nor a shipment establishes full utilization. Commissioning release, operating target

The 28,000-tonne figure needs particular care. Company wording describes matte product tonnes; a management interview separately described approximately 21,000 tonnes of nickel within 27,000–28,000 tonnes of matte. The original table should therefore not silently treat 28,000 as contained nickel. Management also discussed product flexibility between ferronickel and matte, which establishes an operating option, not simultaneous production of both. Management interview, September 2025

MMP and Mitsui signed their memorandum on 8 December 2025. The announcement covers marketing, investment exploration and longer-term business development, with Mitsui assisting Japanese and global access. It does not disclose completed equity participation, an exclusive output commitment or a fixed contract term. The practical logic is a domestic producer combining its plant with an international trading company’s customer network; the eventual refinery still requires shipment-level or customer evidence. MMP–Mitsui announcement

7. BMS: ferronickel operation and a historical sulfate memorandum

BMS was established in October 2014 within Kalla, a diversified Indonesian group. Company photographs and reporting substantiate ferronickel tapping. The parent’s business page identifies the ferronickel plant as operating and the battery-grade nickel-sulfate plant as under construction. These are sufficient to establish FeNi, but not commercial production of either matte grade or sulfate. BMS, Kalla project description

There is a real historical POSCO connection. The December 2021 memorandum was described in a company statement relayed by Indonesian media as envisaging POSCO taking all nickel-sulfate output, together with exploration of precursor/cathode development at Luwu. JOGMEC subsequently recorded the memorandum and the intended initial export of sulfate before a fuller domestic chain. This should be retained as a historical full-sulfate-output arrangement or intention, not evidence of years of executed deliveries. Contemporaneous statement, JOGMEC follow-up

The contemplated product was sulfate, not all ferronickel output or an interchangeable matte stream. Public information reviewed does not establish the agreement’s duration, price, minimum purchases or implementation date. These unknowns do not prevent a clear current classification: FeNi confirmed; other products assessed separately.

8. Ceria and ANTAM: domestic resources, different project structures

Ceria

Ceria’s official profile describes incorporation in 1991, acquisition of the Lapaopao concession in 2011 and domestic ownership. Its move into smelting extends a mine into higher-value processing. Government records document the 2019 groundbreaking and continued construction discussions in July 2024. The company’s own 2025 update identifies first ferronickel on 27 April. A large multi-stage project announced years earlier should not all be counted as commissioned capacity. Company profile, 2019 government record, 2024 progress, first-production company statement

Reporting in June 2024 described discussions over Glencore investment, HPAL financing and future offtake. That is not a closing announcement. No reviewed document supports treating Glencore as an established owner or exclusive buyer of current ferronickel. HPAL, matte refining and battery-material ambitions need their own stage assessments. Investment discussions

ANTAM

ANTAM, within MIND ID, combines gold, nickel, bauxite and other mineral businesses. Its traditional nickel activities include ore sales and ferronickel. The FY2024 investor presentation distinguishes commercial Kolaka from commissioning-stage East Halmahera. The 2025 operating report describes ferronickel export markets in major stainless-steel-producing countries. Neither corporate involvement in nickel nor a new battery strategy makes its established plants matte producers. FY2024 presentation, p.5, FY2025 operating report, p.4

The November 2022 CNGR framework concerns Pomalaa, KIAT and the proposed PNEM OESBF matte project, with two phases totaling 80,000 tonnes of contained nickel annually. Its original 2025 completion aspiration is not a commissioning record. ANTAM’s ore resources and CNGR’s processing/materials position explain the partnership, while contemplated cross-investment requires subsequent completion evidence. ANTAM–CNGR framework

The separate CBL ecosystem—CATL, Brunp and Lygend—involves East Halmahera ore, RKEF and HPAL alongside downstream facilities connected to Karawang. ANTAM’s FY2025 disclosure reports the June 2025 launch and EPC activity beginning in the fourth quarter. That supports construction status, not completed MHP or matte output. FY2025 project update, p.5

ANTAM’s 2024 annual report also describes Gag Nikel’s acquisition of 30% of JLMI, a specific resource–smelting investment. It cannot be generalized into an ANTAM interest in every Tsingshan project. Ore supply, matte offtake and battery-material purchases are separate contracts; terms were not inferred from group relationships. 2024 annual report

9. Implications for the project database

Keep separate fields for actual product, planned product, feed, parent, ownership date, contractual customer, consignee, final processor and agreement evidence. Vale supports numerical offtake shares and an updated 2026 payability. Jinchuan supports associated supply and identified downstream uses. MMP supports Mitsui market cooperation. BMS supports a historical sulfate memorandum. Nicole’s own matte production and several construction-stage processing projects still require direct evidence.

These distinctions explain the supply chain better than a single aggregate of every announced “Indonesian matte capacity”: an established integrated system, related-party feed, independent refinery purchases, domestic plants using trading-company networks, and battery projects still under development.

06 Export quantities, shifts and explanations

Sample coverage and measurement

All shipment figures below come from the supplied 1,192-record dataset and can be traced to the companion workbook. Raw input weight is 1,533,705.8247 physical tonnes. The main measure is 1,532,224.5447 tonnes after separately retaining, but excluding from the main total, one 1,481.28-tonne Vale record inferred from price. Two decimals support reconciliation; the interpretation does not depend on small tonnage differences.

The 1.532 Mt figure is the total of the associated shipment sample, not Indonesia’s complete verified matte exports and not entirely high-grade matte. It includes 1.185 Mt classified as high-grade-associated, 0.333 Mt of low-grade candidates, 13.884 kt with conflicting HS and description evidence, and 0.402 kt of sulphide material with unresolved form. Original assays cover about 61.96% of main-measure weight. Nickel contained in that subset is not the nickel total for the entire sample.

The source notes incomplete October 2025 coverage and a Walhsu gap in August 2026. Data end on 29 August 2026; no estimates fill these gaps. July 2026 contains only 15 records across the sample. That is not proof of missing shipments, but identical calendar windows cannot guarantee identical capture rates. A zero in this dataset does not establish zero actual production or sales.

Recorded shipments rose about 11.8% in 2024 and fell about 8.1% in 2025. Missing coverage, changing exporter headings and new customer channels prevent those changes from being read directly as national supply growth or contraction.

January–July 2026 recorded shipments rose by 85,055.92 physical tonnes, or 44.6%. High-grade-associated material increased from 139,940.95 to 242,364.63 tonnes, while low-grade candidates fell from 50,711.89 to 33,461.56 tonnes. This is a change in the captured product mix, not proof of an equivalent production switch. Local consumption, cross-border conversion, different sales channels and capture rates also matter.

The five largest positive contributors, HNMI, NNI, USMI, Jiaman and Zhongtsing, added about 99.77 kt, more than the net increase because other suppliers declined. HNMI had no captured shipments in the previous window and NNI had only 19.66 tonnes. These low bases must not be interpreted as equivalent multiples of installed capacity growth.

What explains the changes and what remains unproven

HNMI: the company directly disclosed its 2025 suspension and October restart following an improved contract. That supports the direction of renewed shipments, but earlier reported production without matching sample exports proves incomplete channel coverage. The 2026 increment is not all new capacity. Quarterly production and sales also moved in opposite directions.

Zhongtsing: phase-two integration in 2026 provides an operating basis for greater supply. Captured January–July shipments rose 53.9%. However, the plant’s shipment mix and disputed historical records prevent allocating all growth to high-grade matte. CNGR’s December 2025 stake increase was an ownership event, not automatically an equivalent physical capacity increase.

NNI: named Indonesian high-grade production was achieved in November 2024. Later export growth is consistent with commercial expansion, but the preceding 19.66-tonne sample base cannot measure the actual ramp-up rate.

Jiaman: the July 2025 commercial sales licence precedes the first captured August shipment. Cumulative sample shipments are 39,094.37 physical tonnes, all to the two recorded spellings of Weiming Shengqing. That establishes a concentrated associated channel. Shengqing’s associate status and the reported direct low-grade-refining route prevent treating the flow as either consolidated internal sales or exclusively local high-grade output.

Vale: January–July sample shipments fell by 10,585.52 tonnes. Q1 disclosures identify the Furnace 3 rebuild and RKAB effects, with later recovery commentary. Those events match the relevant period but do not quantify every tonne of the decline. The move to 82% payability affects pricing, not assay or production volume.

Westrong: a 21.7% sample decline coincides with reported Q2 maintenance. Previous disclosure also establishes sales to Indonesian refiners. Production and domestic/export allocation both matter; the evidence does not assign the entire export decline to maintenance or identify all domestic customers.

Walhsu and Hengsheng: declines of 54.0% and 56.7% are observed, but no full production, inventory and sales bridge establishes their causes. Hengsheng has 3,318.83 tonnes of August 2026 sample shipments after weak mid-year dispatches, so the series is not a permanent cessation. Missing assays remain unresolved.

Lancon and Cosan: one fell 36.0% and the other rose 184.4%. Opposite movements do not prove transferred orders or changed exporter headings. Group operating evidence is insufficient to allocate each plant’s conversion and sales mix.

USMI: the 143.7% increase is consistent with stronger dispatches, and a named converter operating account supports real production. No complete monthly operating bridge establishes the precise cause of the 17,043.51-tonne increment.

Nicole: zero records under the heading in 2026 do not establish closure, especially given continuing company revenue and NPI activity. The five 2025 high-grade shipments also do not establish continuous own production.

Sunny and Debonair: disappearing cross-border low-grade records cannot override direct production evidence. Domestic conversion, group channels and capture differences remain possible. This is why the domestic processing map is retained alongside the export map.

07 Purchase agreements, offtake and execution

Research cutoff:17 September2026. Contract type, product, ownership and execution are assessed separately. Repeated shipments do not establish multi-year offtake; equity percentages are not offtake allocations, and capacity is not contracted volume. Unknown means unavailable in the reviewed material, not that no agreement exists. This appendix synthesises the sources already reviewed in the project chapters.

C01 Vale Indonesia—Vale Canada / Sumitomo Metal Mining

Disclosure/agreement date: 1 July 2024; 29 April 2026 pricing update.

  • Instrument: Shareholder-linked long-term offtake
  • Product: Sorowako high-grade nickel matte
  • Quantity/allocation: Approximately80% Vale Canada and20% SMM; SMM retained its20% annual purchase rights and obligations after its ownership fell to11.48%.
  • Term: Full expiry and renewal provisions not obtained.
  • Pricing: 2026 is the first full year with82% payability. This is not nickel assay; full deductions and cobalt adjustments remain unavailable.
  • Execution evidence: Ongoing production and shipment evidence; Vale Japan may be a consignee rather than the contractual customer.
  • Buyer mechanism: Resource investment integrated with overseas refining; SMM has compatible MCLE facilities.
  • Unresolved: Latest complete amendments, termination provisions and shipment-specific downstream destinations.
  • Next useful verification: Read the latest offtake amendments and revenue notes; separate contractual buyer, consignee and final refinery.

Sources: 2024-07-01 p1; 2024 sustainability report customers; 2026Q1 release p1.

C02 Nickel Industries—Shanghai Decent

Disclosure/agreement date: 2025 annual report.

  • Instrument: NPI offtake commitment
  • Product: NPI; not all non-NPI nickel products
  • Quantity/allocation: Shanghai Decent commits to purchase all NPI production.
  • Term: Full term not obtained.
  • Pricing: Pricing formula not obtained.
  • Execution evidence: Confirmed in the annual report; final consuming plants are not fully allocated.
  • Buyer mechanism: Project development, construction, equity and park infrastructure support the supply arrangement.
  • Unresolved: Plant allocations, specifications, pricing and remedies.
  • Next useful verification: Check cooperation schedules and plant-level NPI revenue notes; do not extend this commitment to all matte.

Sources: 2025 annual report p25.

C03 Nickel Industries—Glencore AG

Disclosure/agreement date: Announced 12 December 2023; deliveries from January 2024.

  • Instrument: Direct matte sales contract
  • Product: Nickel matte
  • Quantity/allocation: Confidential.
  • Term: Initial six months from January2024; renewal through2026 unverified.
  • Pricing: Confidential.
  • Execution evidence: Contract signing disclosed; current terms and fulfilled volume not established.
  • Buyer mechanism: Management explicitly cited western-market access and customer diversification.
  • Unresolved: Fulfilled quantity, renewals, current term and exclusivity.
  • Next useful verification: Review post-mid2024 renewals and sales notes; do not allocate all Hengjaya-heading shipments to Glencore.

Sources: 2023-12-12 company announcement.

C04 Jiaman—Weiming Shengqing; Wenzhou Jiazhan trading channel

Disclosure/agreement date: April 2023; 15 April 2024; May 2026 processing report.

  • Instrument: Priority-supply strategic agreement and later annual signing target
  • Product: High-grade matte in2023/2024 arrangements; low-grade direct refining separately reported in2026.
  • Quantity/allocation: Intended2024 minimum10,000 contained-nickel tonnes and USD126m value; not actual delivery.
  • Term: 2024 target year disclosed; multi-year duration unknown.
  • Pricing: Headline value divided by tonnage is not a verified pricing formula.
  • Execution evidence: Qualified high-grade output and commercial licensing confirmed in2025; signing does not establish2024 deliveries.
  • Buyer mechanism: Feed security for Wenzhou refining; Shengqing is a non-controlling associate despite54.55% ownership.
  • Unresolved: Deferral, low-grade substitution, specifications and processing charges.
  • Next useful verification: Obtain performance and amendment records and low-grade specifications; reconcile Jiazhan and Shengqing receipts.

Sources: 2023Q1 strategic cooperation; 2024-04-15 signing report; Antaike site visit May18–19; Weiming2026H1 p186.

C05 BMS—POSCO

Disclosure/agreement date: 21 December 2021.

  • Instrument: Historical MoU describing intended offtake
  • Product: Proposed nickel sulphate, not FeNi or high-grade matte
  • Quantity/allocation: Reported as all proposed sulphate output.
  • Term: Unknown.
  • Pricing: Unknown.
  • Execution evidence: Continuous sulphate delivery unverified; existing FeNi production is separate.
  • Buyer mechanism: Extension into battery materials, initially exporting sulphate and exploring precursor/cathode activities.
  • Unresolved: Definitive contract, legal enforceability, sulphate commissioning and first delivery.

C11 Zhongtsing/NNI/Debonair/Jade Bay—CNGR buyer platforms

Disclosure/agreement date: See V18 control-date corrections; Qinzhou announcement 31 July 2023.

  • Instrument: Controlled-group feed channel; individual long-term sales terms unavailable
  • Product: Low- or high-grade matte depending on project and period
  • Quantity/allocation: No common disclosed full-offtake percentage.
  • Term: Unknown.
  • Pricing: Unknown.
  • Execution evidence: Weda Bay feed to Qinzhou is publicly confirmed, without complete allocation among individual Indonesian entities.
  • Buyer mechanism: International procurement and domestic material-feed integration; trading buyer is not the final plant.
  • Unresolved: Conversion allocation between China and Indonesia, salt/electrowinning end use and external-sales share.
  • Next useful verification: Match plant receipts to period-specific control; classify Qinzhou-converted high-grade matte as Chinese output.

Sources: 2023-07-31 Qinzhou processing; CNGR2026H1 pp164–172.

Verification priorities and completion criteria

First: evidence that changes product or geographic classification. Hengsheng requires named high-grade first-output, assay or production records. Sunny/Walhsu requires assets and handovers assigned to the correct legal entity. CNGR requires separate Indonesian dispatch and Qinzhou conversion accounting. Financing news and unassayed exports cannot substitute for these records.

Second: contracts that change the transaction mechanism. Huake/Youshan title and processing terms determine purchase-and-resale versus tolling. Jiaman/Shengqing requires evidence of deferred high-grade targets and permitted low-grade substitution. Hengsheng requires examination of any loan-linked purchase covenant. Until then, retain the operational route, associated supply or minority-shareholder channel without inventing full offtake.

Third: whether historical agreements remain effective. The initial NIC/Glencore six-month period is historical and needs renewal evidence. BMS/POSCO and MMP/Mitsui require evidence of transition from MoU to definitive execution. A later shipment cannot establish every provision of an earlier memorandum.

Fourth: volume and pricing details. Vale already provides a20% allocation and82% payability; full amendments remain useful. Elsewhere establish signing, product and execution before pursuing minimum quantities and price coefficients. Undisclosed commercial provisions need not prevent a well-supported production classification.

Verification is complete only when named parties, product, effective period/status and supporting source align. Generic strategic-cooperation language, unnamed park news and undifferentiated revenue cannot close plant-level gaps. Maintain distinct fields for importer, final use and agreement type so that a trading channel is not mistaken for a refinery.

The V18 classification incorporates the corrected control timeline: CNGR controlled Debonair and Jade Bay by July 2022, so subsequent sales to controlled CNGR buyers are common-control channels. CNGR’s 15% holding in Hengsheng does not establish control. Weiming Shengqing remains a disclosed non-controlled associate despite 54.55% ownership. V17 is retained as a historical version.

The common-control share is not a final captive-consumption ratio. Group traders may resell, and independent customers may buy under long-term contracts. This classification concerns ownership and role at the time of trade. The companion workbook identifies all 84 buyer headings, their procurement rationale and limitations, and all 250 relationships with original record identifiers.

Industry conclusions

First, matte capacity is not wholly separate from the NPI resource base. Some nickel changes product destination on existing ore and RKEF infrastructure; other projects directly produce low-grade matte through routes such as oxygen-enriched side blowing. Capacity should be recorded by stage but contained nickel must not be counted twice. Huake–Youshan, Sunny–Walhsu, MBMA’s RKEF plants–HNMI, and CNGR’s Indonesia–China chain illustrate different geographic and legal boundaries between stages.

Second, dependable supply requires feedstock, workable processing, margins and commercial arrangements. HNMI demonstrates that installed capacity is not inflexible output. Vale demonstrates enduring shareholder offtake. Nickel Industries demonstrates that comprehensive NPI offtake can coexist with third-party matte sales. A specific contractual obligation requires a disclosed agreement; repeated shipments establish continuity of trade, not the undisclosed contract terms.

Third, China remains a processing centre, but delivery to China can mean several things. Low-grade feed can first be upgraded to high-grade matte; high-grade feed can enter nickel-salt or metal production; trading entities may arrange onward sales. Country of destination, corporate group and final consuming plant are therefore distinct analytical dimensions.

Fourth, matte and MHP compete for some of the same refining and battery-material demand, but their projects cannot be combined as operating high-grade matte capacity. Cobalt follows the actual recovery route and assays. The supplied shipment data cannot quantify cobalt output. Downstream switching between sulphate and metal also prevents a one-for-one reading of matte growth as battery-grade sulphate growth.

Fifth, the sample supports relationships and product-mix analysis but cannot independently reconstruct national production and exports. Its January–July 2026 volume is 85,055.92 physical tonnes, or 44.59%, above the same calendar window of 2025. This remains a sample observation. HNMI’s production before its observed shipment dates, plant-level missing records and uneven assay coverage all require the qualification to remain attached to totals and growth rates.

Priority unresolved questions

Priority Unresolved point Why it matters Evidence that would resolve it
High Whether Nicole manufactures its exported high-grade matte Operating-plant classification versus a demonstrated export heading Named converter commissioning and actual output, feed and product records
High Whether Hengsheng’s high-grade conversion stage has achieved sustained output Date of transition from low-grade producer to integrated producer Qualified product, quarterly production or customer acceptance rather than construction alone
High Contracts and actual splits for Huake–Youshan and Sunny–Walhsu Outright sales versus toll conversion; removal of duplicate nickel counts Named sales or processing contracts, related-party notes or plant material balances
High Final refineries behind trading platforms Nickel-salt, metal or resale demand Domestic receipt, named-plant purchases and production records rather than port or registered address
Medium Sustained operation of new side-blowing and conversion facilities Ignition does not demonstrate continuous supply Consecutive-quarter feed, production, acceptance and delivery records
Medium Lancon ownership, Huaxing project scope and the Huashuo candidate identity Ownership assignment and project deduplication Formal corporate records, project codes and equipment scope
Medium Production, inventories, domestic sales and channel shifts behind export declines Avoiding the assumption that shipments equal production Contemporaneous production–sales–inventory data and exporter changes
Medium Current purchase quantities, duration and price formulas Future feed allocation and margin sensitivity Effective contracts or named issuer disclosures, not MoUs or financing amounts

These gaps do not invalidate the confirmed plant classifications or observed trades, but they constrain more precise supply forecasts. The report does not assume full utilisation of nominal capacity and does not reconstruct undisclosed private contract terms. Evidence is preserved by version; future updates should retain the applicable ownership and product dates rather than overwrite historical conclusions.
